arXiv:2510.23254v1 [stat.ML] 27 Oct 2025
Provable test-time adaptivity and distributional robustness of in-context learning
Tianyi Ma
Affiliation: Statistical Laboratory, University of Cambridge
Tengyao Wang
Affiliation: Department of Statistics, London School of Economics and Political Science
Richard J. Samworth
Affiliation: Statistical Laboratory, University of Cambridge
Abstract
We study in-context learning problems where a Transformer is pretrained on tasks drawn from a mixture distribution π=∑α∈𝒜λα​πα\pi=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\lambda_{\alpha}\pi_{\alpha}, called the pretraining prior, in which each mixture component πα\pi_{\alpha} is a distribution on tasks of a specific difficulty level indexed by α\alpha. Our goal is to understand the performance of the pretrained Transformer when evaluated on a different test distribution μ\mu, consisting of tasks of fixed difficulty β∈𝒜\beta\in\mathcal{A}, and with potential distribution shift relative to πβ\pi_{\beta}, subject to the chi-squared divergence χ2​(μ,πβ)\chi^{2}(\mu,\pi_{\beta}) being at most κ\kappa. In particular, we consider nonparametric regression problems with random smoothness, and multi-index models with random smoothness as well as random effective dimension. We prove that a large Transformer pretrained on sufficient data achieves the optimal rate of convergence corresponding to the difficulty level β\beta, uniformly over test distributions μ\mu in the chi-squared divergence ball. Thus, the pretrained Transformer is able to achieve faster rates of convergence on easier tasks and is robust to distribution shift at test time. Finally, we prove that even if an estimator had access to the test distribution μ\mu, the convergence rate of its expected risk over μ\mu could not be faster than that of our pretrained Transformers, thereby providing a more appropriate optimality guarantee than minimax lower bounds.
1 Introduction
Transformers (Vaswani et al., 2017) have emerged as one of the dominant architectures in modern machine learning, as they have achieved state-of-the-art performance in many domains such as natural language processing (Devlin et al., 2019; Brown et al., 2020), computer vision (Dosovitskiy et al., 2021; Liu et al., 2021) and protein prediction (Jumper et al., 2021).
A striking ability of pretrained Transformers, first observed by Brown et al. (2020) in Large Language Models (LLMs), is the phenomenon of in-context learning (ICL): given a prompt containing examples and a query, Transformers can learn the underlying pattern from the examples and produce accurate output for the query, without updating its parameters.
For instance, given the values of the function (x,y)↦x​y2(x,y)\mapsto xy^{2} or ‘subfield↦field\text{subfield}\mapsto\text{field}’ at a few distinct points, LLMs are able to recover the value of the function at a new point:
f(1,1)=1,f(1,2)=4,f(2,2)=8,f(2,3)=18,⏟examples​f⁡(3,3)=⏟query⏞prompt​27⏟output,\displaystyle\overbrace{\underbrace{f(1,1)=1,\;f(1,2)=4,\;f(2,2)=8,\;f(2,3)=18,}_{\text{examples}}\;\underbrace{f(3,3)=}_{\text{query}}}^{\text{prompt}}\underbrace{27\vphantom{f(2,2)}}_{\text{output}},
(1)
Genetics→Biology,Relativity→Physics,⏟examples​Topology→⏟query⏞prompt​Mathematics⏟output.\displaystyle\overbrace{\underbrace{\text{Genetics}\to\text{Biology},\;\text{Relativity}\to\text{Physics},}_{\text{examples}}\;\underbrace{\text{Topology}\to}_{\text{query}}}^{\text{prompt}}\underbrace{\text{Mathematics}\vphantom{y}}_{\text{output}}.
The ICL ability of Transformers was also exploited by Hollmann et al. (2025) to build tabular foundation models, which they show to outperform many classical machine learning methods, such as tree-based and boosting algorithms, on regression and classification problems for tabular data.
Extensive studies on ICL have been conducted from various perspectives (Garg et al., 2022; Xie et al., 2022; Von Oswald et al., 2023; Bai et al., 2023; Kim, Nakamaki and Suzuki, 2024, e.g.); we refer the readers to Section 1.1 for a more detailed literature review. However, most theoretical analyses of ICL have focused on the setting where the distribution of pretraining data (which we call the pretraining prior) and the distribution of the test data (which we call the test distribution) are the same. In practice, Transformers are pretrained on a variety of tasks of different difficulties, and the test distribution will typically exhibit distribution shift from the pretraining prior. For example, we may train a Transformer on translation tasks between different languages, where Dutch–English translation is typically easier than Chinese–English, since Dutch and English are both West Germanic languages with shared structure. At test time, the Transformer is required to translate between two specific languages, while some linguistic patterns may appear more frequently in the test data than in the pretraining data. To formulate a theoretical framework, we focus on regression problems (in line with most prior work) and consider a Transformer pretrained on a mixture distribution containing tasks of different difficulties, but where the test distribution consists of tasks of a specific difficulty with potential distribution shift from the corresponding mixture component of the pretraining prior. We study the following natural but important questions:
Do pretrained Transformers adapt to the difficulty of the test distribution, and are they robust to distribution shift?
Our contributions
•
In Proposition 1, we provide a key test-time risk upper bound, which decomposes into a term that captures both the degree of distribution shift and the proximity of the pretrained Transformer to the posterior regression function, and another term that represents the rate of convergence of the posterior regression function to the true regression function.
•
In Section 3.2, we prove a universal approximation theorem for Transformers with softmax attention and non-polynomial activation in FFN layers. We further show that a large Transformer pretrained on sufficient data can learn the posterior regression function arbitrarily well when the activation in FFN layers is one of 𝖱𝖾𝖫𝖴\mathsf{ReLU}, 𝖦𝖤𝖫𝖴\mathsf{GELU} or 𝖲𝗂𝖫𝖴\mathsf{SiLU}.
•
In Sections 4.1 and 4.2, we consider general nonparametric regression problems and multi-index models respectively, with regression functions belonging to Besov smoothness classes. We prove that the posterior regression function is optimally adaptive to the difficulty of every task in the support of the pretraining prior (and hence robust to distribution shift). In combination with the results mentioned above, this establishes the adaptivity and distributional robustness of pretrained Transformers.
•
Finally, in Section 4.3, we show that the expected risk of any estimator over the test distribution μ\mu cannot achieve a faster rate of convergence than our pretrained Transformers (even if the estimator is allowed to depend on μ\mu). This provides a more appropriate form of optimality guarantee for pretrained Transformers than that yielded by minimax lower bounds usually considered in ICL literature.
1.1 Related work
In-context learning
There is a large body of theoretical research that aims to explain the ICL ability of Transformers.
One line of work shows that Transformers can perform ICL by approximating certain algorithms, such as gradient descent (Ahn et al., 2023; Akyürek et al., 2023; Bai et al., 2023; Von Oswald et al., 2023), functional gradient descent (Cheng, Chen and Sra, 2024), basis function regression (Kim, Nakamaki and Suzuki, 2024), reinforcement learning (Lin, Bai and Mei, 2024) and domain adaptation (Hataya, Matsui and Imaizumi, 2024). Other lines of work analyse ICL through the lens of Bayesian inference (Xie et al., 2022; Zhang et al., 2022; Nagler, 2023; Wang et al., 2023; Deora et al., 2025; Zhang et al., 2025), meta-learning (Dai et al., 2023; Jeon et al., 2024; Wu, Wang and Yao, 2025) and training dynamics (Oko et al., 2024; Zhang, Frei and Bartlett, 2024; Kumano, Kera and Yamasaki, 2025; Kuwataka and Suzuki, 2025). In particular, Bai et al. (2023) show that Transformers can perform gradient descent on penalised linear regression, generalised linear models and two-layer neural networks, as well as implement in-context algorithm selection, while Kim, Nakamaki and Suzuki (2024) prove that Transformers are minimax optimal for nonparametric regression. Both papers consider the case where the test distribution coincides with the pretraining prior. On the other hand, Xie et al. (2022) study the setting where the test prompts have different formats compared to the pretraining data, and provide consistency results under a Bayesian hidden Markov model. There are also numerous empirical studies on the behaviour and properties of ICL (Garg et al., 2022; Kirsch et al., 2022; Panwar, Ahuja and Goyal, 2024; Reuter et al., 2025, e.g.). Finally, we note the concurrent and independent work of Wakayama and Suzuki (2025), who study the adaptivity of ICL from a Bayesian perspective. Their results are complementary to ours, and neither subsumes the other.
Posterior contraction theory
Posterior contraction theory is an active area of research in statistics that studies the concentration of the posterior distribution around a true data generating distribution (i.e. from a frequentist perspective). Pioneered by Ghosal, Ghosh and van der Vaart (2000) and Shen and Wasserman (2001), posterior contraction for Bayesian nonparametric models has been investigated in different problems (Ghosal, Lember and van der Vaart, 2008; van der Vaart and van Zanten, 2009; Finocchio and Schmidt-Hieber, 2023; Egels and Castillo, 2025, e.g.). In this paper, we modify the tools developed by Ghosal, Lember and van der Vaart (2008) to derive rates of convergence for the posterior regression function. Interested readers are referred to the book by Ghosal and van der Vaart (2017) for an introduction to the field.
1.2 Notation
For n∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}, we write [n]≔{1,…,n}[n]\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,n\}. For m,n∈ℕm,n\in\mathbb{N} with m<nm<n, we write [m:n]≔{m,m+1,…,n}[m:n]\coloneqq\{m,m+1,\ldots,n\} and similarly [n:∞)≔{n,n+1,…}[n:\infty)\coloneqq\{n,n+1,\ldots\}. Given a,b∈ℝa,b\in\mathbb{R}, we write a∧b≔min⁡{a,b}a\wedge b\coloneqq\min\{a,b\} and a∨b≔max⁡{a,b}a\vee b\coloneqq\max\{a,b\}. For d∈ℕd\in\mathbb{N}, we define 𝟎d≔(0,…,0)⊤∈ℝd\bm{0}_{d}\coloneqq(0,\ldots,0)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} and 𝟏d≔(1,…,1)⊤∈ℝd\bm{1}_{d}\coloneqq(1,\ldots,1)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}. For R>0R>0, we define the clipping operator 𝖼𝗅𝗂𝗉R:ℝ→[−R,R]\mathsf{clip}_{R}:\mathbb{R}\to[-R,R] by 𝖼𝗅𝗂𝗉R​(x)≔−R∨x∧R\mathsf{clip}_{R}(x)\coloneqq-R\vee x\wedge R for x∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}. Given a Borel measurable set A⊆ℝdA\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}, we write Vold​(A)\mathrm{Vol}_{d}(A) for the dd-dimensional Lebesgue measure of AA.
For two σ\sigma-finite measures PP and QQ on a measurable space (𝒳,Σ)(\mathcal{X},\Sigma), we say that PP is absolutely continuous with respect to QQ, written as P≪QP\ll Q, if P⁡(A)=0P(A)=0 whenever A∈ΣA\in\Sigma is such that Q⁡(A)=0Q(A)=0, and in that case write d​Pd​Q\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q} for the Radon–Nikodym derivative. When PP and QQ are probability measures, the χ2\chi^{2}-divergence from QQ to PP is defined as
χ2​(P,Q)≔{∫𝒳(d​Pd​Q)2​𝑑Q−1if ​P≪Q∞otherwise.\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P,Q)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\bigl(\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}Q-1\quad&\text{if }P\ll Q\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}
Further, we write P⊗nP^{\otimes n} for the nn-fold product measure of PP and supp⁡(P)\mathrm{supp}(P) for the support of PP, i.e. the intersection of all closed A∈ΣA\in\Sigma with P⁡(A)=1P(A)=1. For a set ℱ\mathcal{F} of functions from 𝒳\mathcal{X} to 𝒴\mathcal{Y} and a set 𝒢\mathcal{G} of functions from 𝒴\mathcal{Y} to 𝒵\mathcal{Z}, we write ℱ∘𝒢≔{f∘g:f∈ℱ,g∈𝒢}\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\{f\circ g:f\in\mathcal{F},\,g\in\mathcal{G}\}, where f∘gf\circ g denotes the composition of ff and gg. For a function f:𝒳→ℝf:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}, we write ‖f‖∞≔supx∈𝒳|f⁡(x)|\|f\|_{\infty}\coloneqq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|f(x)|.
2 Problem set-up and ICL excess risk decomposition
We first present the generating mechanism of our pretraining data. Let 𝒳⊆ℝd\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d} and 𝒴⊆ℝ\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathbb{R} be Borel measurable, and let 𝒫\mathcal{P} be a measurable space of probability distributions on 𝒳×𝒴\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}, dominated by a common σ\sigma-finite measure. The pretraining prior π\pi is a distribution on 𝒫\mathcal{P}, and our pretraining data take the form (𝒟n(t),X(t),Y(t))t=1T(\mathcal{D}_{n}^{(t)},X^{(t)},Y^{(t)})_{t=1}^{T}, generated as follows:
1.
Draw random distributions P1,…,PT​∼iid​πP_{1},\ldots,P_{T}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\pi.
2.
For each t∈[T]t\in[T], draw 𝒟n(t)≔(Xi(t),Yi(t))i=1n∼Pt⊗n\mathcal{D}_{n}^{(t)}\coloneqq(X_{i}^{(t)},Y_{i}^{(t)})_{i=1}^{n}\sim P_{t}^{\otimes n} and (X(t),Y(t))∼Pt(X^{(t)},Y^{(t)})\sim P_{t} independently.
Thus, for each t∈[T]t\in[T], 𝒟n(t)\mathcal{D}_{n}^{(t)} consists of nn examples, X(t)X^{(t)} is our query and our goal is to predict its corresponding output Y(t)Y^{(t)}; see (1). We emphasise that the test distribution μ\mu on 𝒫\mathcal{P} of new prompts at test time will typically be different from the (possibly unknown) distribution π\pi that generated our pretraining data.
Next, for a class ℱ\mathcal{F} of measurable functions from 𝒳×(𝒳×𝒴)n\mathcal{X}\times(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n} to 𝒴\mathcal{Y} (e.g. Transformers), define the empirical risk minimiser f^T\widehat{f}_{T} over ℱ\mathcal{F} by
f^T∈argminf∈ℱℛ^T​(f),where ​ℛ^T​(f)≔1T​∑t=1T{Y(t)−f⁡(X(t),𝒟n(t))}2\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{T}\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{T}(f),\quad\text{where }\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{T}(f)\coloneqq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bigl\{Y^{(t)}-f(X^{(t)},\mathcal{D}_{n}^{(t)})\bigr\}^{2}
(2)
and (𝒟n(t),X(t),Y(t))t=1T(\mathcal{D}_{n}^{(t)},X^{(t)},Y^{(t)})_{t=1}^{T} are generated according to π\pi.
We remark that, given a new set of examples Dn∈(𝒳×𝒴)nD_{n}\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}, the prediction at a query x∈𝒳x\in\mathcal{X} is given by f^T​(x,Dn)\widehat{f}_{T}(x,D_{n}), and we do not need to update f^T\widehat{f}_{T} accordingly.
For a distribution ν\nu on 𝒫\mathcal{P}, we define the ν\nu-risk of a measurable g:𝒳×(𝒳×𝒴)n→𝒴g:\mathcal{X}\times(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}\to\mathcal{Y} as11
1
We adopt the convention that
for P∈𝒫P\in\mathcal{P}, 𝔼P\mathbb{E}_{P} means that the random variables inside the expectation are generated from PP, i.e. (𝒟n,X,Y)∼P⊗(n+1)(\mathcal{D}_{n},X,Y)\sim P^{\otimes(n+1)}, whereas for a distribution ν\nu on 𝒫\mathcal{P}, 𝔼ν​𝔼P\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\mathbb{E}_{P} means that the distribution PP is also random and drawn from ν\nu.
ℛν​(g)\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\nu}(g)

If f~T\widetilde{f}_{T} is random and depends on the pretraining data (𝒟n(t),X(t),Y(t))t=1T(\mathcal{D}_{n}^{(t)},X^{(t)},Y^{(t)})_{t=1}^{T}, then we interpret ℛν​(f~T)\mathcal{R}_{\nu}(\widetilde{f}_{T}) as the expectation conditional on the pretraining data, which is assumed to be independent of the test data (𝒟n,X,Y)(\mathcal{D}_{n},X,Y), so ℛν​(f~T)\mathcal{R}_{\nu}(\widetilde{f}_{T}) is also random. For a test distribution μ\mu on 𝒫\mathcal{P}, we further define the ICL excess μ\mu-risk of f~T\widetilde{f}_{T} by
ℛμICL​(f~T)\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{ICL}}_{\mu}(\widetilde{f}_{T})
≔𝔼​ℛμ​(f~T)−𝔼μ​𝔼P​[{Y−𝔼P​(Y|X)}2]\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}_{\mu}(\widetilde{f}_{T})-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl[\bigl\{Y-\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y\,|\,X)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr]
=𝔼​𝔼μ​𝔼P​[{f~T​(X,𝒟n)−𝔼P​(Y|X)}2],\displaystyle\hskip 2.56064pt=\mathbb{E}\,\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl[\bigl\{\widetilde{f}_{T}(X,\mathcal{D}_{n})-\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y\,|\,X)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr],
(3)
where the term 𝔼μ​𝔼P​[{Y−𝔼P​(Y|X)}2]\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl[\bigl\{Y-\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y\,|\,X)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr] is the oracle risk assuming knowledge of the distribution PP that generates (X,Y)(X,Y), and in particular of the true regression function 𝔼P​(Y|X)\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y\,|\,X) under PP, and the equality is shown in the proof of Proposition 1.
Finally, we define the posterior regression function gπ:𝒳×(𝒳×𝒴)n→𝒴g_{\pi}:\mathcal{X}\times(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}\to\mathcal{Y} (with respect to π\pi) by
gπ​(x,Dn)\displaystyle g_{\pi}(x,D_{n})
≔𝔼π​𝔼P​(Y|X=x,𝒟n=Dn)\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl(Y\,|\,X=x,\,\mathcal{D}_{n}=D_{n}\bigr)
(4)
for x∈𝒳x\in\mathcal{X} and Dn∈(𝒳×𝒴)nD_{n}\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}. Thus gπ​(x,Dn)g_{\pi}(x,D_{n}) is the posterior mean of YY given X=xX=x and 𝒟n=Dn\mathcal{D}_{n}=D_{n}, where (𝒟n,X,Y)|P∼P⊗(n+1)(\mathcal{D}_{n},X,Y)\,|\,P\sim P^{\otimes(n+1)} and P∼πP\sim\pi.
The following proposition provides a key upper bound on the ICL excess μ\mu-risk, enabling us to study the adaptivity and distributional robustness of ICL.
Proposition 1.
Let R>0R>0, and suppose that for all P∈𝒫P\in\mathcal{P}, we have |𝔼P​(Y|X)|≤R|\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y\,|\,X)|\leq R almost surely.
Let f^T\widehat{f}_{T} be defined as in (2), let f~T≔𝖼𝗅𝗂𝗉R∘f^T\widetilde{f}_{T}\coloneqq\mathsf{clip}_{R}\circ\widehat{f}_{T} and let ℰ⁡(f~T)≔𝔼​ℛπ​(f~T)−ℛπ​(gπ)\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{f}_{T})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}_{\pi}(\widetilde{f}_{T})-\mathcal{R}_{\pi}(g_{\pi}). Then
ℛμICL​(f~T)≤4​R​{χ2​(μ,π)+1}​ℰ​(f~T)+2​𝔼μ​𝔼P​[{gπ​(X,𝒟n)−𝔼P​(Y|X)}2].\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{ICL}}_{\mu}(\widetilde{f}_{T})\leq 4R\sqrt{\bigl\{\chi^{2}(\mu,\pi)+1\bigr\}\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{f}_{T})}+2\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl[\bigl\{g_{\pi}(X,\mathcal{D}_{n})-\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y\,|\,X)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr].
(5)
The upper bound in (5) consists of two terms. The first involves both the extent of the distribution shift measured by the χ2\chi^{2}-divergence, as well as the expected difference between the π\pi-risk of f~T\widetilde{f}_{T} and that of the posterior regression function gπg_{\pi}. It is well-known that gπg_{\pi} minimises the π\pi-risk ℛπ\mathcal{R}_{\pi} over all measurable functions, so intuitively, if f~T\widetilde{f}_{T} is a large Transformer pretrained on a sufficient corpus of data, then ℰ⁡(f~T)\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{f}_{T}) should be negligible. Indeed, this is proved in our Proposition 3. The second term reflects the proximity of the posterior regression function to the true regression function. In fact, in Sections 4.1 and 4.2, we will control 𝔼P​[{gπ​(X,𝒟n)−𝔼P​(Y|X)}2]\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl[\bigl\{g_{\pi}(X,\mathcal{D}_{n})-\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y\,|\,X)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr] uniformly for all PP in the support of μ\mu. This reveals the interesting feature that only the first term on the right-hand side of (5) depends on the degree of distribution shift, and this term can be made arbitrarily small by increasing the size of the Transformer and the pretraining dataset, so that the second term ultimately determines the rate of convergence.
3 Transformers, universal approximation and learnability
3.1 Transformers
In this section, we formally define the classes of (encoder-only) Transformers that we will consider.
For a vector v=(v1,…,vd)⊤∈ℝdv=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}, let 𝖲𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑⁡(v)∈ℝd\softmax(v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d} denote the vector whose jjth entry is given by exp⁡(vj)/∑ℓ=1dexp⁡(vℓ)\exp(v_{j})/\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\exp(v_{\ell}). Often, we will apply the 𝖲𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑\softmax function to matrices, by which we mean that the 𝖲𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑\softmax function is applied row-wise. Given dmodel≥d+2d_{\mathrm{model}}\geq d+2, called the model dimension, examples Dn=(xi,yi)i=1n∈(𝒳×𝒴)nD_{n}=(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n} and a query x∈𝒳x\in\mathcal{X}, the input matrix is defined as
Zin≡Zin​(Dn,x)≔(x1⊤y1𝟎dmodel−d−2⊤0xn⊤yn𝟎dmodel−d−2⊤0x⊤0𝟎dmodel−d−2⊤1)∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodel,\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{in}}\equiv Z_{\mathrm{in}}(D_{n},x)\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{\top}&y_{1}&\bm{0}_{d_{\mathrm{model}}-d-2}^{\top}&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
x_{n}^{\top}&y_{n}&\bm{0}_{d_{\mathrm{model}}-d-2}^{\top}&0\\
x^{\top}&0&\bm{0}_{d_{\mathrm{model}}-d-2}^{\top}&1\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}},
(6)
where the last column of ZinZ_{\mathrm{in}} is our positional encoding used to identify the query, and we pad each row with zeros to make them dmodeld_{\mathrm{model}}-dimensional. A Transformer iteratively applies attention layers and feed forward network (FFN) layers to the input matrix ZinZ_{\mathrm{in}}. Letting N∈ℕN\in\mathbb{N} denote an upper bound on the context length, our universal approximation theory (Theorem 2) holds uniformly for all n∈[N]n\in[N], so it is convenient to consider Transformers as functions from22
2
The space ⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}} is equipped with the disjoint union topology and its corresponding Borel σ\sigma-algebra. ⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}} to ⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}.
We now define attention layers.
Definition 1 (Attention layer).
Let H∈ℕH\in\mathbb{N} and for h∈[H]h\in[H], let Qh,Kh,Vh∈ℝdmodel×dmodelQ_{h},K_{h},V_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{model}}\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}. The attention layer with HH heads and parameters θattn≔(Qh,Kh,Vh)h=1H\theta_{\mathrm{attn}}\coloneqq(Q_{h},K_{h},V_{h})_{h=1}^{H} is the function 𝖠𝗍𝗍𝗇θattn:⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel→⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel\mathsf{Attn}_{\theta_{\mathrm{attn}}}:\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}\to\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}} such that for n∈[N]n\in[N] and Z∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodelZ\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}},
𝖠𝗍𝗍𝗇θattn⁡(Z)\displaystyle\attn_{\theta_{\mathrm{attn}}}(Z)
≔Z+∑h=1H𝖲𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑⁡(ZQh​Kh⊤​Z⊤dmodel)​ZVh∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodel.\displaystyle\coloneqq Z+\sum_{h=1}^{H}\softmax\biggl(\frac{ZQ_{h}K_{h}^{\top}Z^{\top}}{\sqrt{d_{\mathrm{model}}}}\biggr)ZV_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}.
Definition 2 (FFN layer).
Let dffn∈ℕd_{\mathrm{ffn}}\in\mathbb{N}, W1∈ℝdmodel×dffnW_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{model}}\times d_{\mathrm{ffn}}}, W2∈ℝdffn×dmodelW_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{ffn}}\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}, v∈ℝdffnv\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{ffn}}} and let ρ:ℝ→ℝ\rho:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}. The FFN layer with parameters θffn≔(W1,W2,v)\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}\coloneqq(W_{1},W_{2},v) (and activation function ρ\rho) is the function 𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn:⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel→⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}}:\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}\to\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}} such that for n∈[N]n\in[N] and Z∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodelZ\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}},
𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn​(Z)≔Z+ρ⁡(Z​W1+𝟏n+1​v⊤)​W2∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodel,\displaystyle\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}}(Z)\coloneqq Z+\rho(ZW_{1}+\bm{1}_{n+1}v^{\top})W_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}},
where ρ\rho is applied entrywise.
Now, a Transformer block is a composition of an attention layer and an FFN layer.
Definition 3 (Transformer block).
Let θattn\theta_{\mathrm{attn}} and θffn\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}} be as in Definitions 1 and 2 respectively. The Transformer block with parameters (θattn,θffn)(\theta_{\mathrm{attn}},\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}) is the function 𝖳𝖥𝖡𝗅𝗈𝖼𝗄(θattn,θffn):⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel→⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel\mathsf{TFBlock}_{(\theta_{\mathrm{attn}},\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}})}:\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}\to\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}} given by
𝖳𝖥𝖡𝗅𝗈𝖼𝗄(θattn,θffn)≔𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn∘𝖠𝗍𝗍𝗇θattn.\displaystyle\mathsf{TFBlock}_{(\theta_{\mathrm{attn}},\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}})}\coloneqq\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}}\circ\attn_{\theta_{\mathrm{attn}}}.
We say that the Transformer block 𝖳𝖥𝖡𝗅𝗈𝖼𝗄(θattn,θffn)\mathsf{TFBlock}_{(\theta_{\mathrm{attn}},\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}})} has HH heads, model dimension dmodeld_{\mathrm{model}} and FFN width dffnd_{\mathrm{ffn}}.
Finally, a Transformer is a composition of Transformer blocks.
Definition 4 (Transformers).
We define ℱTF​(L,H,dmodel,dffn)\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TF}}(L,H,d_{\mathrm{model}},d_{\mathrm{ffn}}) to be the set of all Transformers
𝖳𝖥:⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel→⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel\mathsf{TF}:\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}\to\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}} of the form
𝖳𝖥=𝖳𝖥𝖡𝗅𝗈𝖼𝗄(L)∘𝖳𝖥𝖡𝗅𝗈𝖼𝗄(L−1)∘⋯∘𝖳𝖥𝖡𝗅𝗈𝖼𝗄(1),\displaystyle\mathsf{TF}=\mathsf{TFBlock}^{(L)}\circ\mathsf{TFBlock}^{(L-1)}\circ\cdots\circ\mathsf{TFBlock}^{(1)},
where 𝖳𝖥𝖡𝗅𝗈𝖼𝗄(ℓ)≡𝖳𝖥𝖡𝗅𝗈𝖼𝗄(θattn(ℓ),θffn(ℓ))\mathsf{TFBlock}^{(\ell)}\equiv\mathsf{TFBlock}_{(\theta_{\mathrm{attn}}^{(\ell)},\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{(\ell)})} is a Transformer block with HH heads, model dimension dmodeld_{\mathrm{model}} and FFN width dffnd_{\mathrm{ffn}} for all ℓ∈[L]\ell\in[L].
For a Transformer 𝖳𝖥∈ℱTF​(L,H,dmodel,dffn)\mathsf{TF}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TF}}(L,H,d_{\mathrm{model}},d_{\mathrm{ffn}}), examples Dn=(xi,yi)i=1nD_{n}=(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n} and a query xx, we will slightly abuse our notation by writing 𝖳𝖥⁡(x,Dn)≔𝖳𝖥⁡(Zin​(Dn,x))\mathsf{TF}(x,D_{n})\coloneqq\mathsf{TF}\bigl(Z_{\mathrm{in}}(D_{n},x)\bigr) where the input matrix Zin​(Dn,x)Z_{\mathrm{in}}(D_{n},x) is defined by (6). Finally, we define 𝖱𝖾𝖺𝖽:⋃n=1Nℝ(n+1)×dmodel→ℝ\mathsf{Read}:\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}\to\mathbb{R} by 𝖱𝖾𝖺𝖽⁡(Z)≔Zn+1,d+1\mathsf{Read}(Z)\coloneqq Z_{n+1,d+1} for Z∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodelZ\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}} and n∈[N]n\in[N]. Thus, 𝖱𝖾𝖺𝖽∘𝖳𝖥⁡(x,Dn)\mathsf{Read}\circ\mathsf{TF}(x,D_{n}) can be interpreted as the predicted value of the response at the query xx based on examples DnD_{n}.
3.2 Universal approximation and learnability
We now consider pretraining priors induced by randomly drawn regression functions, and prove universal approximation and learnability results for Transformers.
Let 𝒢\mathcal{G} be a measurable space of real-valued, measurable functions on 𝒳⊆ℝd\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d} that are uniformly bounded by R>0R>0 and let π~\widetilde{\pi} be a distribution on 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Let XX be a random variable on 𝒳\mathcal{X} with distribution PXP_{X}, and let ξ⟂⟂X\xi\perp\!\!\!\perp X be a zero-mean random variable on ℝ\mathbb{R}. For g∈𝒢g\in\mathcal{G}, let PgP_{g} be the distribution of (X,Yg)(X,Y_{g}) where Yg=g⁡(X)+ξY_{g}=g(X)+\xi. Further let π\pi be the distribution of the random measure Pg~P_{\widetilde{g}}, where the randomness comes from g~∼π~\widetilde{g}\sim\widetilde{\pi}. Suppose throughout this subsection that there exist R′>0R^{\prime}>0 and a σ\sigma-finite measure ν\nu on ℝ\mathbb{R} such that for all g∈𝒢g\in\mathcal{G} and almost all realisations of g⁡(X)g(X), the conditional distribution of Yg|g⁡(X)Y_{g}\,|\,g(X) is absolutely continuous with respect to ν\nu, with Radon–Nikodym derivative bounded by R′R^{\prime}. For example, if the noise ξ\xi has a N⁡(0,σ2)N(0,\sigma^{2}) distribution, then the conditional distribution of Yg|{g(X)=z}Y_{g}\,|\,\{g(X)=z\} has bounded Lebesgue density given by y↦12​π​σ2e−(y−z)2/(2σ2)y\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}e^{-(y-z)^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}. The following theorem shows that Transformers are universal approximators for the posterior regression function gπg_{\pi} given by (4).
Theorem 2.
Suppose that the activation function ρ:ℝ→ℝ\rho:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} in the FFN layers is Borel measurable and there is no polynomial hh such that ρ=h\rho=h Lebesgue almost everywhere. Then for any ϵ>0\epsilon>0 and N∈ℕN\in\mathbb{N}, there exist dmodel≥d+2d_{\mathrm{model}}\geq d+2, dffn∈ℕd_{\mathrm{ffn}}\in\mathbb{N} and a Transformer 𝖳𝖥∈ℱTF​(3,1,dmodel,dffn)\mathsf{TF}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TF}}(3,1,d_{\mathrm{model}},d_{\mathrm{ffn}}) such that
maxn∈[N]⁡𝔼P∼π,(𝒟n,X,Y)|P∼P⊗(n+1)​{(𝖱𝖾𝖺𝖽∘𝖳𝖥⁡(X,𝒟n)−gπ​(X,𝒟n))2}≤ϵ.\displaystyle\max_{n\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{P\sim\pi,(\mathcal{D}_{n},X,Y)|P\sim P^{\otimes(n+1)}}\Bigl\{\bigl(\mathsf{Read}\circ\mathsf{TF}(X,\mathcal{D}_{n})-g_{\pi}(X,\mathcal{D}_{n})\bigr)^{2}\Bigr\}\leq\epsilon.
From now on, we will assume that the activation ρ\rho belongs to the set {𝖱𝖾𝖫𝖴,𝖦𝖤𝖫𝖴,𝖲𝗂𝖫𝖴}\{\mathsf{ReLU},\mathsf{GELU},\mathsf{SiLU}\}, which are commonly used activation functions in modern Transformer models; see (14) for their definitions.
The following proposition shows that, when the noise ξ\xi is sub-Gaussian, a large Transformer pretrained with sufficient data can achieve a π\pi-risk that is arbitrarily close to the π\pi-risk of gπg_{\pi}. Note that we do not require the input matrix or the parameters of the Transformer to be bounded. We achieve this by showing that the pseudo-dimension of the class is finite using results from a branch of mathematical logic called model theory.
Proposition 3.
Suppose that the noise ξ\xi is sub-Gaussian. For any n∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N} and ϵ>0\epsilon>0, there exist dmodel∘≥d+2d_{\mathrm{model}}^{\circ}\geq d+2 and dffn∘∈ℕd_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{\circ}\in\mathbb{N} such that the following holds. Suppose dmodel≥dmodel∘d_{\mathrm{model}}\geq d_{\mathrm{model}}^{\circ}, dffn≥dffn∘d_{\mathrm{ffn}}\geq d_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{\circ}, and f^T\widehat{f}_{T} is defined as in (2) with ℱ≔𝖱𝖾𝖺𝖽∘ℱTF​(3,1,dmodel,dffn)\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\mathsf{Read}\circ\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TF}}(3,1,d_{\mathrm{model}},d_{\mathrm{ffn}}) and pretraining data generated according to π\pi. Let f~T≔𝖼𝗅𝗂𝗉R∘f^T\widetilde{f}_{T}\coloneqq\mathsf{clip}_{R}\circ\widehat{f}_{T}. Then, for all TT sufficiently large,
ℰ⁡(f~T)≔𝔼​ℛπ​(f~T)−ℛπ​(gπ)≤ϵ.\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{f}_{T})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}_{\pi}(\widetilde{f}_{T})-\mathcal{R}_{\pi}(g_{\pi})\leq\epsilon.
This proposition confirms formally the intuition provided after Proposition 1 that the first term in the decomposition (5) can be made negligible.
4 Adaptivity and robustness of ICL
In this section, we consider mixture distribution pretraining priors π=∑α∈𝒜λα​πα\pi=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\lambda_{\alpha}\pi_{\alpha} for some finite index set 𝒜\mathcal{A}, and test distribution μ\mu on 𝒫\mathcal{P} with χ2​(μ,πβ)≤κ\chi^{2}(\mu,\pi_{\beta})\leq\kappa for some β∈𝒜\beta\in\mathcal{A}, so that draws from μ\mu contain tasks of difficulty β\beta with potential distribution shift relative to πβ\pi_{\beta}. We have already seen that Proposition 1 provides a decomposition of the ICL excess μ\mu-risk, and Proposition 3 shows that the first term on the right-hand side of (5) can be made negligible. Our goal here, then, is to control the key quantity in the other term, namely
𝔼P​[{gπ​(X,𝒟n)−𝔼P​(Y|X)}2]\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl[\bigl\{g_{\pi}(X,\mathcal{D}_{n})-\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y\,|\,X)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr], uniformly over all PP in the support of πβ\pi_{\beta}. To this end, we specialise the general posterior contraction theory for nonparametric regression that we provide in Appendix E.2 to Besov regression functions with random smoothness (in Section 4.1) and multi-index models (in Section 4.2).
4.1 In-context learning for Besov functions
For the remainder of this section, we take33
3
Our results in this section extend to any compact domain 𝒳⊆ℝd\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d} by a scaling argument. 𝒳≔[0,1]d\mathcal{X}\coloneqq[0,1]^{d}, 𝒴≔ℝ\mathcal{Y}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}. Since Besov functions can be characterised by their wavelet decomposition, we will consider regression functions with random wavelet coefficients. Let L2​([0,1]d)L^{2}([0,1]^{d}) denote the set of square integrable functions on [0,1]d[0,1]^{d}, let S∈ℕS\in\mathbb{N} and let
{Φk:k∈K}∪{Ψℓ,γ:ℓ∈[ℓ0:∞),γ∈Γℓ}\displaystyle\bigl\{\Phi_{k}:k\in K\bigr\}\cup\bigl\{\Psi_{\ell,\gamma}:\ell\in[{\ell_{0}}:\infty),\,\gamma\in\Gamma_{\ell}\bigr\}
(7)
denote the tensor product Cohen–Daubechies–Vial (CDV) wavelet basis for L2​([0,1]d)L^{2}([0,1]^{d}), constructed from SS-regular and SS-times continuously differentiable wavelets. The precise definitions of these wavelet functions are not important for us, and we will only use some basic properties of this wavelet basis, summarised in Appendix D.2. Let c0∈(0,1]c_{0}\in(0,1] and C0≥1C_{0}\geq 1.
For α∈(0,S)\alpha\in(0,S), let π~α\widetilde{\pi}_{\alpha} be the distribution of the random function
g~α≔∑k∈KC02−ℓ0d/2ak(α)Φk+∑ℓ=ℓ0∞∑γ∈ΓℓC02−ℓ⁡(α+d/2)bℓ,γ(α)Ψℓ,γ,\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{\alpha}\coloneqq\sum_{k\in K}C_{0}2^{-\ell_{0}d/2}a_{k}^{(\alpha)}\Phi_{k}+\sum_{\ell=\ell_{0}}^{\infty}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\ell}}C_{0}2^{-\ell(\alpha+d/2)}b_{\ell,\gamma}^{(\alpha)}\Psi_{\ell,\gamma},
(8)
where (ak(α),bℓ,γ(α):k∈K,ℓ∈[ℓ0:∞),γ∈Γℓ)\bigl(a_{k}^{(\alpha)},b_{\ell,\gamma}^{(\alpha)}:k\in K,\ell\in[\ell_{0}:\infty),\gamma\in\Gamma_{\ell}\bigr) are independent random variables supported on [−1,1][-1,1] with Lebesgue density bounded between c0/2c_{0}/2 and c0−1/2c_{0}^{-1}/2. The scaling of the wavelet coefficients in (8) is chosen to ensure that g~α\widetilde{g}_{\alpha} belongs to the Besov space B∞,∞α​([0,1]d)B_{\infty,\infty}^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d}) with smoothness α\alpha. Indeed, a function g≔∑k∈Kak′​Φk+∑ℓ=ℓ0∞∑γ∈Γℓbℓ,γ′​Ψℓ,γg\coloneqq\sum_{k\in K}a_{k}^{\prime}\Phi_{k}+\sum_{\ell=\ell_{0}}^{\infty}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\ell}}b_{\ell,\gamma}^{\prime}\Psi_{\ell,\gamma}, with deterministic wavelet coefficients, belongs to the Besov ball B∞,∞α​([0,1]d,C)B_{\infty,\infty}^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d},C) of radius C>0C>0 if and only if 2ℓ0​d/2​maxk​|ak′|+supℓ,γ2ℓ⁡(α+d/2)​|bℓ,γ′|≤C2^{\ell_{0}d/2}\max_{k}|a_{k}^{\prime}|+\sup_{\ell,\gamma}2^{\ell(\alpha+d/2)}|b_{\ell,\gamma}^{\prime}|\leq C; see Appendix D. Thus, writing 𝒢α≔supp⁡(π~α)\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}\coloneqq\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{\pi}_{\alpha}), we have
B∞,∞α​([0,1]d,C0)⊆𝒢α⊆B∞,∞α​([0,1]d,2​C0).\displaystyle B_{\infty,\infty}^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d},C_{0})\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}\subseteq B_{\infty,\infty}^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d},2C_{0}).
(9)
We remark that the Besov space B∞,∞α​([0,1]d)B_{\infty,\infty}^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d}) coincides with the Hölder space Hα​([0,1]d)H^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d}) for α∉ℕ\alpha\notin\mathbb{N}, and it contains Hα​([0,1]d)H^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d}) for α∈ℕ\alpha\in\mathbb{N}; see Appendix D.1 for more details. Moreover, there exists Cα,S,d>0C_{\alpha,S,d}>0 such that supg∈𝒢α‖g‖∞≤Cα,S,d\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}}\|g\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\alpha,S,d}; see (54).
Now let PXP_{X} be a Borel probability distribution on [0,1]d[0,1]^{d} with the property that there exists a hypercube A⊆[0,1]dA\subseteq[0,1]^{d} such that PX​(A0)≥c0​Vold​(A0)P_{X}(A_{0})\geq c_{0}\mathrm{Vol}_{d}(A_{0}) for all measurable A0⊆AA_{0}\subseteq A. Let X∼PXX\sim P_{X} and ξ∼N⁡(0,σ2)\xi\sim N(0,\sigma^{2}) be independent. For a Borel measurable function g:[0,1]d→ℝg:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}, let PgP_{g} denote the distribution of (X,Yg)(X,Y_{g}) where Yg=g⁡(X)+ξY_{g}=g(X)+\xi. We then define πα\pi_{\alpha} to be the distribution of the random measure Pg~αP_{\widetilde{g}_{\alpha}}, where the randomness comes from the random regression function g~α∼π~α\widetilde{g}_{\alpha}\sim\widetilde{\pi}_{\alpha}, and let 𝒫α≔supp⁡(πα)\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}\coloneqq\mathrm{supp}(\pi_{\alpha}). Letting 𝒜⊆(0,S)\mathcal{A}\subseteq(0,S) be a finite set and letting (λα)α∈𝒜(\lambda_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}} be a sequence of positive weights such that ∑α∈𝒜λα=1\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\lambda_{\alpha}=1, our pretraining prior π\pi is defined to be the mixture distribution
π≔∑α∈𝒜λα​πα.\displaystyle\pi\coloneqq\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\lambda_{\alpha}\pi_{\alpha}.
(10)
Thus π\pi consists of random regression tasks where the smoothness of the regression functions is randomly drawn from 𝒜\mathcal{A} with probabilities given by (λα)α∈𝒜(\lambda_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}.
Finally, we define 𝒫≔⋃α∈𝒜𝒫α\mathcal{P}\coloneqq\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{P}_{\alpha} and 𝒢≔⋃α∈𝒜𝒢α\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}. By (9), the set 𝒢\mathcal{G} contains a union of Besov balls with smoothness parameters in 𝒜\mathcal{A}.
The following proposition provides the rate of convergence of the posterior regression, when the true regression belongs to 𝒢β\mathcal{G}_{\beta} for some β∈𝒜\beta\in\mathcal{A}.
Proposition 4.
For any β∈𝒜\beta\in\mathcal{A} and g∘∈𝒢βg^{\circ}\in\mathcal{G}_{\beta}, write P0≡Pg∘P_{0}\equiv P_{g^{\circ}}, let π\pi be the prior distribution defined in (10) and let gπg_{\pi} denote the posterior regression function with respect to π\pi. Then there exists C>0C>0, not depending on nn, such that
𝔼(𝒟n,X,Y)∼P0⊗(n+1)[{gπ(X,𝒟n)−g∘(X)}2]≤Cn−2β/(2β+d)\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(\mathcal{D}_{n},X,Y)\sim P_{0}^{\otimes(n+1)}}\bigl[\bigl\{g_{\pi}(X,\mathcal{D}_{n})-g^{\circ}(X)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr]\leq Cn^{-2\beta/(2\beta+d)}
for all n∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}.
Combining Propositions 1, 3 and 4, we are now ready to state the main theorem of this section.
Theorem 5.
For any n∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N} and κ>0\kappa>0,
there exist dmodel∘≥d+2d_{\mathrm{model}}^{\circ}\geq d+2 and dffn∘∈ℕd_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{\circ}\in\mathbb{N} such that the following holds. Suppose dmodel≥dmodel∘d_{\mathrm{model}}\geq d_{\mathrm{model}}^{\circ}, dffn≥dffn∘d_{\mathrm{ffn}}\geq d_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{\circ}, f^T\widehat{f}_{T} is defined as in (2) with ℱ≔𝖱𝖾𝖺𝖽∘ℱTF​(3,1,dmodel,dffn)\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\mathsf{Read}\circ\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TF}}(3,1,d_{\mathrm{model}},d_{\mathrm{ffn}}) and pretraining prior π\pi defined in (10). Let R≔supg∈𝒢‖g‖∞R\coloneqq\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\|g\|_{\infty} and f~T≔𝖼𝗅𝗂𝗉R∘f^T\widetilde{f}_{T}\coloneqq\mathsf{clip}_{R}\circ\widehat{f}_{T}. Then, for all TT sufficiently large and β∈𝒜\beta\in\mathcal{A},
supμ:χ2​(μ,πβ)≤κℛμICL(f~T)≤Cn−2β/(2β+d),\displaystyle\sup_{\mu\,:\,\chi^{2}(\mu,\pi_{\beta})\leq\kappa}\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{ICL}}_{\mu}(\widetilde{f}_{T})\leq Cn^{-2\beta/(2\beta+d)},
where C>0C>0 does not depend on nn and κ\kappa.
The Transformer f~T\widetilde{f}_{T} in Theorem 5 is pretrained on the mixture distribution π=∑α∈𝒜λα​πα\pi=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\lambda_{\alpha}\pi_{\alpha} with different smoothness levels, whereas the test distribution μ\mu consists of random regression functions of a fixed smoothness level β\beta, with a distribution shift such that χ2​(μ,πβ)≤κ\chi^{2}(\mu,\pi_{\beta})\leq\kappa. When we evaluate the performance of f~T\widetilde{f}_{T} under the test distribution μ\mu, Theorem 5 shows that the ICL excess μ\mu-risk of f~T\widetilde{f}_{T} is adaptive to the unknown smoothness β\beta, in the sense that its rate of convergence n−2β/(2β+d)n^{-2\beta/(2\beta+d)} is optimal even if β\beta were known; see Theorem 8. Moreover, this risk bound holds uniformly over test distributions in a χ2\chi^{2}-divergence ball around πβ\pi_{\beta}. Thus, although the Transformer is pretrained on tasks of different levels of difficulties, it is able to achieve faster (and optimal) rates of convergence on easier tasks (without knowing the difficulty of the tasks) and is robust to distribution shift.
Finally, we remark that dmodel∘,dffn∘d_{\mathrm{model}}^{\circ},d_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{\circ} and TT should increase with κ\kappa in order to keep CC independent of κ\kappa. Thus, for a Transformer with fixed architecture, fine tuning for downstream tasks may still be beneficial in cases where (i) the Transformer is not large enough, (ii) the amount of pretraining data is not sufficient, or (iii) there is significant distribution shift (κ\kappa is large).
4.2 In-context learning for multi-index models
To avoid the curse of dimensionality in the previous section, we will now consider multi-index models with44
4
Again, our results in this section extend to any compact domain 𝒳⊆ℝd\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d} by a scaling argument. 𝒳={x∈ℝd:‖x‖2≤1}≕𝔹d\mathcal{X}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|x\|_{2}\leq 1\}\eqqcolon\mathbb{B}^{d} and 𝒴=ℝ\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}. For p∈[d]p\in[d], let Vp​(ℝd)≔{U∈ℝd×p:U⊤​U=Ip}V_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\coloneqq\bigl\{U\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}:U^{\top}U=I_{p}\bigr\} denote the set of all projection matrices from ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d} to ℝp\mathbb{R}^{p} (also called the Stiefel manifold). We will consider regression functions on 𝔹d\mathbb{B}^{d} of the form x↦g⁡((U⊤​x+𝟏p)/2)x\mapsto g\bigl((U^{\top}x+\bm{1}_{p})/2\bigr), where U∈Vp​(ℝd)U\in V_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) for some effective dimension p∈[d]p\in[d] and Borel measurable g:[0,1]p→ℝg:[0,1]^{p}\to\mathbb{R}. Note that we translate and scale U⊤​xU^{\top}x to ensure that it belongs to [0,1]p[0,1]^{p}.
For α∈(0,S)\alpha\in(0,S) and p∈[d]p\in[d], let g~α(p):[0,1]p→ℝ\widetilde{g}_{\alpha}^{(p)}:[0,1]^{p}\to\mathbb{R} be the random function (with smoothness α\alpha) defined as in (8), but with dd replaced by pp in the wavelet coefficients, and using the tensor product CDV wavelet basis for L2​([0,1]p)L^{2}([0,1]^{p}) instead of L2​([0,1]d)L^{2}([0,1]^{d}). Let π~α,p\widetilde{\pi}_{\alpha,p} be the distribution of the random function g~α,p:𝔹d→ℝ\widetilde{g}_{\alpha,p}:\mathbb{B}^{d}\to\mathbb{R} defined by
g~α,p​(x)≔g~α(p)​((U(p))⊤​x+𝟏p2),\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{\alpha,p}(x)\coloneqq\widetilde{g}_{\alpha}^{(p)}\biggl(\frac{(U^{(p)})^{\top}x+\bm{1}_{p}}{2}\biggr),
(11)
where U(p)U^{(p)} is uniformly distributed55
5
One way to generate such a random matrix is to set U(p)≔Z(Z⊤Z)−1/2U^{(p)}\coloneqq Z(Z^{\top}Z)^{-1/2}, where ZZ is a d×pd\times p random matrix with independent N⁡(0,1)N(0,1) entries (e.g. Chikuse, 2003, Theorem 2.2.1(iii)). on the Stiefel manifold Vp​(ℝd)V_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}), independently of g~α(p)\widetilde{g}_{\alpha}^{(p)}. We write 𝒢α,p\mathcal{G}_{\alpha,p} for the support of π~α,p\widetilde{\pi}_{\alpha,p}.
Next, let PXP_{X} be a distribution on 𝔹d\mathbb{B}^{d} with the property that there exist c0>0c_{0}>0 and a non-empty Euclidean ball A⊆𝔹dA\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{d} such that PX​(A0)≥c0​Vold​(A0)P_{X}(A_{0})\geq c_{0}\mathrm{Vol}_{d}(A_{0}) for all measurable A0⊆AA_{0}\subseteq A.
We then define πα,p\pi_{\alpha,p} to be the distribution of the random measure Pg~α,pP_{\widetilde{g}_{\alpha,p}}, where the randomness comes from the random regression function g~α,p∼π~α,p\widetilde{g}_{\alpha,p}\sim\widetilde{\pi}_{\alpha,p}, and let 𝒫α,p≔supp⁡(πα,p)\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,p}\coloneqq\mathrm{supp}(\pi_{\alpha,p}). For a finite set 𝒜′⊆(0,S)×[d]\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq(0,S)\times[d] and a sequence of positive weights (λα,p)(α,p)∈𝒜′(\lambda_{\alpha,p})_{(\alpha,p)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}} such that ∑(α,p)∈𝒜′λα,p=1\sum_{(\alpha,p)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}\lambda_{\alpha,p}=1, our pretraining prior π\pi for multi-index models is defined to be the mixture distribution
π≔∑(α,p)∈𝒜′λα,p​πα,p.\displaystyle\pi\coloneqq\sum_{(\alpha,p)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}\lambda_{\alpha,p}\pi_{\alpha,p}.
(12)
Thus π\pi generates random multi-index regression tasks, where the smoothness and effective dimensions of the regression functions are randomly drawn from 𝒜′\mathcal{A}^{\prime} with probabilities given by (λα,p)(α,p)∈𝒜′(\lambda_{\alpha,p})_{(\alpha,p)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}.
Finally, we define 𝒫≔⋃(α,p)∈𝒜′𝒫α,p\mathcal{P}\coloneqq\bigcup_{(\alpha,p)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,p} and 𝒢≔⋃(α,p)∈𝒜′𝒢α,p\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\bigcup_{(\alpha,p)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha,p}. Proposition 6 is the analogue for multi-index models of Proposition 4.
Proposition 6.
For any (β,r)∈𝒜′(\beta,r)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime} and g∘∈𝒢β,rg^{\circ}\in\mathcal{G}_{\beta,r}, write P0≡Pg∘P_{0}\equiv P_{g^{\circ}}, let π\pi be the prior distribution defined in (12) and let gπg_{\pi} denote the posterior regression function with respect to π\pi. Then there exists C>0C>0, not depending on nn, such that
𝔼(𝒟n,X,Y)∼P0⊗(n+1)[{gπ(X,𝒟n)−g∘(X)}2]≤Cn−2β/(2β+r)\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(\mathcal{D}_{n},X,Y)\sim P_{0}^{\otimes(n+1)}}\bigl[\bigl\{g_{\pi}(X,\mathcal{D}_{n})-g^{\circ}(X)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr]\leq Cn^{-2\beta/(2\beta+r)}
for all n∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}.
Combining Propositions 1, 3 and 6, we can now conclude that a pretrained Transformer is adaptive to the effective dimension of the multi-index models, as well as adapting to the smoothness of the regression function and being robust to distribution shift.
Theorem 7.
For any n∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N} and κ>0\kappa>0, there exist dmodel∘≥d+2d_{\mathrm{model}}^{\circ}\geq d+2 and dffn∘∈ℕd_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{\circ}\in\mathbb{N} such that the following holds. Suppose dmodel≥dmodel∘d_{\mathrm{model}}\geq d_{\mathrm{model}}^{\circ}, dffn≥dffn∘d_{\mathrm{ffn}}\geq d_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{\circ}, f^T\widehat{f}_{T} is defined as in (2) with ℱ≔𝖱𝖾𝖺𝖽∘ℱTF​(3,1,dmodel,dffn)\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\mathsf{Read}\circ\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TF}}(3,1,d_{\mathrm{model}},d_{\mathrm{ffn}}) and pretraining prior π\pi defined in (12). Let R≔supg∈𝒢‖g‖∞R\coloneqq\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\|g\|_{\infty} and f~T≔𝖼𝗅𝗂𝗉R∘f^T\widetilde{f}_{T}\coloneqq\mathsf{clip}_{R}\circ\widehat{f}_{T}. Then, for all TT sufficiently large and (β,r)∈𝒜′(\beta,r)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime},
supμ:χ2​(μ,πβ,r)≤κℛμICL(f~T)≤Cn−2β/(2β+r),\displaystyle\sup_{\mu\,:\,\chi^{2}(\mu,\pi_{\beta,r})\leq\kappa}\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{ICL}}_{\mu}(\widetilde{f}_{T})\leq Cn^{-2\beta/(2\beta+r)},
where C>0C>0 does not depend on nn and κ\kappa.
4.3 Lower bounds on the ICL excess risk
Our final theoretical contribution is to provide lower bounds on the ICL excess risk. Prior work has sought to establish the optimality of ICL algorithms via lower bounds on the minimax risk
infg^n∈𝒢^nsupP∈supp⁡(μ)𝔼P​[{g^n​(X,𝒟n)−𝔼P​(Y|X)}2],\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{g}_{n}\in\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\sup_{P\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl[\bigl\{\widehat{g}_{n}(X,\mathcal{D}_{n})-\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y\,|\,X)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr],
(13)
where 𝒢^n\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n} denotes the set of all measurable functions from 𝒳×(𝒳×𝒴)n\mathcal{X}\times(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n} to 𝒴\mathcal{Y}. Although the minimax risk is often regarded as the gold standard in traditional statistical learning problems, the ICL excess μ\mu-risk ℛμICL\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ICL}} is an expectation over P∼μP\sim\mu, not a worst-case risk over P∈supp⁡(μ)P\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu). Thus lower bounds on (13) do not provide lower bounds for the ICL excess μ\mu-risk. Theorem 8 below, however, does indeed provide a lower bound on the ICL excess μ\mu-risk, and in a strong sense: even an estimator that has knowledge of the test distribution μ\mu cannot achieve a better rate of convergence than the pretrained Transformers in Theorems 5 and 7.
Theorem 8.
(a) Under the setting of Section 4.1, let β∈𝒜\beta\in\mathcal{A} and let μ≪πβ\mu\ll\pi_{\beta} be such that supP∈𝒫βd​μd​πβ​(P)<∞\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\beta}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{\beta}}(P)<\infty. Then there exists c>0c>0, not depending on nn, such that
infg^n∈𝒢^nℛμICL(g^n)≥cn−2β/(2β+d).\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{g}_{n}\in\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ICL}}(\widehat{g}_{n})\geq cn^{-2\beta/(2\beta+d)}.
(b) In the setting of Section 4.2, let (β,r)∈𝒜′(\beta,r)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime} and let μ≪πβ,r\mu\ll\pi_{\beta,r} be such that supP∈𝒫β,rd​μd​πβ,r​(P)<∞\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\beta,r}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{\beta,r}}(P)<\infty. Then there exists c′>0c^{\prime}>0, not depending on nn, such that
infg^n∈𝒢^nℛμICL(g^n)≥c′n−2β/(2β+r).\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{g}_{n}\in\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ICL}}(\widehat{g}_{n})\geq c^{\prime}n^{-2\beta/(2\beta+r)}.
We remark that, if f~T\widetilde{f}_{T} is defined as in Theorems 5 or 7, then conditional on the pretraining data, we have f~T∈𝒢^n\widetilde{f}_{T}\in\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n}.

Appendix A Notation for the appendix
Let (𝒫,d)(\mathcal{P},d) be a metric space, ϵ>0\epsilon>0 and 𝒫′⊆𝒫\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}, we write 𝒩⁡(ϵ,𝒫′,d)\mathcal{N}(\epsilon,\mathcal{P}^{\prime},d) for the minimum number of closed ϵ\epsilon-balls in dd needed to cover 𝒫′\mathcal{P}^{\prime}. For m,n∈ℕm,n\in\mathbb{N}, let In∈ℝn×nI_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n} be the identity matrix; for a matrix A∈ℝn×mA\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}, define the operator norm of AA by ∥A∥op≔supx∈ℝm:‖x‖2=1∥Ax∥2\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\coloneqq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\|x\|_{2}=1}\|Ax\|_{2} and define its entrywise ℓ∞\ell_{\infty}-norm by ‖A‖∞≔maxi∈[n],j∈[m]⁡|Ai​j|\|A\|_{\infty}\coloneqq\max_{i\in[n],j\in[m]}|A_{ij}|.
Let PP and QQ be probability measures on 𝒵\mathcal{Z}, and let ν\nu be a measure on 𝒵\mathcal{Z} such that P≪νP\ll\nu and Q≪νQ\ll\nu (such a measure always exists, e.g. ν=P+Q\nu=P+Q). Write p=d​Pd​νp=\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}\nu} and q=d​Qd​νq=\frac{\mathrm{d}Q}{\mathrm{d}\nu}, then the Hellinger distance between PP and QQ is defined as
dH​(P,Q)≔(∫𝒵(p⁡(z)−q⁡(z))2​𝑑ν​(z))1/2.\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}(P,Q)\coloneqq\Bigl(\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\bigl(\sqrt{p(z)}-\sqrt{q(z)}\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu(z)\Bigr)^{1/2}.
The definition is independent of the choice of the dominating measure ν\nu, and dHd_{\mathrm{H}} is a metric on the space of distributions on 𝒵\mathcal{Z}. The Kullback–Leibler divergence from QQ to PP is defined as
KL⁡(P,Q)≔𝔼P​(log⁡d​Pd​Q​(Z))=∫𝒵log⁡d​Pd​Q​(z)​𝑑P​(z)\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(P,Q)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggl(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(Z)\biggr)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(z)\,\mathrm{d}P(z)
if P≪QP\ll Q, and infinity otherwise; this is the PP-expectation of the log-likelihood ratio between PP and QQ. We further define
V2​(P,Q)≔VarP​(log⁡d​Pd​Q​(Z))=∫𝒵(log⁡d​Pd​Q​(z)−KL⁡(P,Q))2​𝑑P​(z)\displaystyle\mathrm{V}_{2}(P,Q)\coloneqq\mathrm{Var}_{P}\biggl(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(Z)\biggr)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\biggl(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(z)-\mathrm{KL}(P,Q)\biggr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}P(z)
if P≪QP\ll Q, and infinity otherwise; this is the PP-variance of the log-likelihood ratio between PP and QQ.
For d∈ℕd\in\mathbb{N}, p∈[1,∞)p\in[1,\infty), a Borel measurable function f:ℝd→ℝf:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R} and a Borel measure μ\mu on ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, we define
‖f‖Lp​(μ)≔(∫ℝd|f⁡(x)|p​𝑑μ​(x))1/p.\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mu)}\coloneqq\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\biggr)^{1/p}.
and write Lp​(μ)L^{p}(\mu) for the set of functions f:ℝd→ℝf:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R} with ‖f‖Lp​(μ)<∞\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mu)}<\infty.
If the domain of ff is restricted to 𝒳⊆ℝd\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d} and μ\mu is the Lebesgue measure restricted to a Borel set 𝒳\mathcal{X}, then we write ‖f‖Lp​(𝒳)≡‖f‖Lp​(μ)\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{X})}\equiv\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mu)} and Lp​(𝒳)≡Lp​(μ)L^{p}(\mathcal{X})\equiv L^{p}(\mu); when 𝒳\mathcal{X} is clear from context, we further write ‖f‖Lp≡‖f‖Lp​(𝒳)\|f\|_{L^{p}}\equiv\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{X})}.
For a zero-mean random variable XX on ℝ\mathbb{R}, we say that XX is sub-Gamma in the right tail with variance parameter σ2>0\sigma^{2}>0 and scale parameter c>0c>0 if for all λ∈[0,1/c)\lambda\in[0,1/c), we have
𝔼​eλ​X≤exp⁡(σ2​λ22​(1−c​λ)).\displaystyle\mathbb{E}e^{\lambda X}\leq\exp\biggl(\frac{\sigma^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2(1-c\lambda)}\biggr).
Similarly, XX is sub-Gamma in the left tail if −X-X is sub-Gamma in the right tail. And we say XX is sub-Gamma in both tails is it is both sub-Gamma in the right tail and sub-Gamma in the left tail.
Finally, we define three commonly used activation functions in Transformers. For x∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}, define
𝖱𝖾𝖫𝖴(x)≔max{0,x},𝖦𝖤𝖫𝖴(x)≔xΦ(x)and𝖲𝗂𝖫𝖴(x)≔x1+e−x,\displaystyle\mathsf{ReLU}(x)\coloneqq\max\{0,x\},\quad\mathsf{GELU}(x)\coloneqq x\Phi(x)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{SiLU}(x)\coloneqq\frac{x}{1+e^{-x}},
(14)
where Φ(x)≔∫−∞x12​πe−t2/2dt\Phi(x)\coloneqq\int_{-\infty}^{x}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-t^{2}/2}\,\mathrm{d}t is the distribution function of standard Gaussian distribution.

Appendix C Proofs for Section 3.2
C.1 Proof of Theorem 2
We work under the assumptions of Section 3.2 and we assume that ρ\rho is not a polynomial. For g∈𝒢g\in\mathcal{G}, (X,Yg)∼Pg(X,Y_{g})\sim P_{g} and z∈ℝz\in\mathbb{R}, let y↦fξ​(y,z)y\mapsto f_{\xi}(y,z) denote the conditional density of Yg|{g(X)=z}Y_{g}\,|\,\{g(X)=z\} with respect to the dominating measure ν\nu. Further let f¯ξ≔fξ/R′\bar{f}_{\xi}\coloneqq f_{\xi}/R^{\prime}.
By Bayes’ theorem and (41), the posterior regression function gπg_{\pi} can be written as
gπ​(x,Dn)=∫𝒢g⁡(x)​∏i=1nf¯ξ​(yi−g⁡(xi))​𝑑π~​(g)∫𝒢∏i=1nf¯ξ​(yi−g⁡(xi))​𝑑π~​(g).\displaystyle g_{\pi}(x,D_{n})=\frac{\int_{\mathcal{G}}g(x)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i}-g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)}{\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i}-g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)}.
(16)
Throughout this section, we assume that ((X1,Y1),…,(XN,YN),(X,Y))|P∼P⊗(N+1)\bigl((X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{N},Y_{N}),(X,Y)\bigr)\,|\,P\sim P^{\otimes(N+1)} with P∼πP\sim\pi. For n∈[N]n\in[N] and dmodel≥d+2d_{\mathrm{model}}\geq d+2, define
Zin(n,dmodel)≔(X1⊤Y1𝟎dmodel−d−2⊤0Xn⊤Yn𝟎dmodel−d−2⊤0X⊤0𝟎dmodel−d−2⊤1)∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodel.\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n,d_{\mathrm{model}})}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}^{\top}&Y_{1}&\bm{0}_{d_{\mathrm{model}}-d-2}^{\top}&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
X_{n}^{\top}&Y_{n}&\bm{0}_{d_{\mathrm{model}}-d-2}^{\top}&0\\
X^{\top}&0&\bm{0}_{d_{\mathrm{model}}-d-2}^{\top}&1\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}.
(17)
We first write down the rows of attention layers and FFN layers before we start.
Consider the notation in Definition 1, if Z∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodelZ\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}} has rows Z1⊤,…,Zn+1⊤∈ℝ1×dmodelZ_{1}^{\top},\ldots,Z_{n+1}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}, then the iith row [𝖠𝗍𝗍𝗇θattn(Z)]i,:[\attn_{\theta_{\mathrm{attn}}}(Z)]_{i,:} of 𝖠𝗍𝗍𝗇θattn⁡(Z)\attn_{\theta_{\mathrm{attn}}}(Z) can be written as
[𝖠𝗍𝗍𝗇θattn(Z)]i,:=Zi⊤+∑h=1H∑j=1n+1Zj⊤Vh⋅exp⁡(Zi⊤​Qh​Kh⊤​Zj/dmodel)∑ℓ=1n+1exp⁡(Zi⊤​Qh​Kh⊤​Zℓ/dmodel)∈ℝ1×dmodel.\displaystyle[\attn_{\theta_{\mathrm{attn}}}(Z)]_{i,:}=Z_{i}^{\top}+\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}Z_{j}^{\top}V_{h}\cdot\frac{\exp(Z_{i}^{\top}Q_{h}K_{h}^{\top}Z_{j}/\sqrt{d_{\mathrm{model}}})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n+1}\exp(Z_{i}^{\top}Q_{h}K_{h}^{\top}Z_{\ell}/\sqrt{d_{\mathrm{model}}})}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}.
Next, consider the notation in Definition 2, if Z∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodelZ\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}} has rows Z1⊤,…,Zn+1⊤∈ℝ1×dmodelZ_{1}^{\top},\ldots,Z_{n+1}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}, then the iith row [𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn(Z)]i,:[\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}}(Z)]_{i,:} of 𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn​(Z)\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}}(Z) can be written as
[𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn(Z)]i,:=(Zi+W2⊤ρ(W1⊤Zi+v))⊤.\displaystyle[\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}}(Z)]_{i,:}=\bigl(Z_{i}+W_{2}^{\top}\rho(W_{1}^{\top}Z_{i}+v)\bigr)^{\top}.
Thus the FFN layer applies a single-hidden layer neural network to each row of ZZ.
Lemma 9.
For any ϵ,δ>0\epsilon,\delta>0, there exist M∈ℕM\in\mathbb{N} and g1,…,gM∈𝒢g_{1},\ldots,g_{M}\in\mathcal{G} such that with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta, we have
maxn∈[N]⁡|1M​∑m=1Mgm​(X)​∏i=1nf¯ξ​(Yi,gm​(Xi))−∫𝒢g⁡(X)​∏i=1nf¯ξ​(yi,g⁡(xi))​𝑑π~​(g)|≤ϵ\displaystyle\max_{n\in[N]}\biggl|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}g_{m}(X)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},g_{m}(X_{i})\bigr)-\int_{\mathcal{G}}g(X)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)\biggr|\leq\epsilon
and
maxn∈[N]⁡|1M​∑m=1M∏i=1nf¯ξ​(Yi,gm​(Xi))−∫𝒢∏i=1nf¯ξ​(yi,g⁡(xi))​𝑑π~​(g)|≤ϵ.\displaystyle\max_{n\in[N]}\biggl|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},g_{m}(X_{i})\bigr)-\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)\biggr|\leq\epsilon.
Proof.
Let M≔2​ϵ−2​(R2∨1)​log⁡(2​N/δ)M\coloneqq 2\epsilon^{-2}(R^{2}\vee 1)\log(2N/\delta) and let g~1,…,g~M​∼iid​π~\widetilde{g}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{g}_{M}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\widetilde{\pi} be independent of (Xi,Yi)i=1N(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i=1}^{N} and (X,Y)(X,Y).
For n∈[N]n\in[N], define events
An≔{|1M∑m=1Mg~m(X)∏i=1nf¯ξ(Yi,g~m(Xi))−∫𝒢g(X)∏i=1nf¯ξ(yi,g(xi))dπ~(g)|≤ϵ}\displaystyle A_{n}\coloneqq\Biggl\{\biggl|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\widetilde{g}_{m}(X)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},\widetilde{g}_{m}(X_{i})\bigr)-\int_{\mathcal{G}}g(X)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)\biggr|\leq\epsilon\Biggr\}
and
Bn≔{|1M∑m=1M∏i=1nf¯ξ(Yi,g~m(Xi))−∫𝒢∏i=1nf¯ξ(yi,g(xi))dπ~(g)|≤ϵ}.\displaystyle B_{n}\coloneqq\Biggl\{\biggl|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},\widetilde{g}_{m}(X_{i})\bigr)-\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)\biggr|\leq\epsilon\Biggr\}.
We have by Hoeffding’s inequality that
ℙ⁡(Anc|X1,Y1,…,Xn,Yn,X)≤δ2​Nandℙ⁡(Bnc|X1,Y1,…,Xn,Yn,X)≤δ2​N.\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\,|\,X_{1},Y_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{n},X)\leq\frac{\delta}{2N}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}(B_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\,|\,X_{1},Y_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{n},X)\leq\frac{\delta}{2N}.
Then by a union bound,
𝔼⁡{ℙ⁡(⋃n=1N(Anc∪Bnc)|g~1,…,g~M)}=ℙ⁡(⋃n=1N(Anc∪Bnc))\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\mathbb{P}\biggl(\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}(A_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\cup B_{n}^{\mathrm{c}})\,\bigg|\,\widetilde{g}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{g}_{M}\biggr)\biggr\}=\mathbb{P}\biggl(\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}(A_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\cup B_{n}^{\mathrm{c}})\biggr)
=𝔼⁡{ℙ⁡(⋃n=1N(Anc∪Bnc)|X1,Y1,…,XN,YN,X,Y)}≤δ.\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\mathbb{P}\biggl(\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}(A_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\cup B_{n}^{\mathrm{c}})\,\bigg|\,X_{1},Y_{1},\ldots,X_{N},Y_{N},X,Y\biggr)\biggr\}\leq\delta.
Therefore,
ℙ{ℙ(⋃n=1N(Anc∪Bnc)|g~1,…,g~M)≤δ}>0.\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl\{\mathbb{P}\biggl(\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}(A_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\cup B_{n}^{\mathrm{c}})\,\biggm|\,\widetilde{g}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{g}_{M}\biggr)\leq\delta\biggr\}>0.
This proves the claim.
∎
Lemma 10.
For all n∈[N]n\in[N], we have
∫𝒢∏i=1nf¯ξ​(yi,g⁡(xi))​𝑑π~​(g)>0\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)>0
almost surely.
Proof.
The density (with respect to ν\nu) of (Y1,…,Yn)(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}) conditional on (X1,…,Xn)(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}) is given by
(y1,…,yn)↦∫𝒢∏i=1nfξ​(yi,g⁡(Xi))​𝑑π~​(g).\displaystyle(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\mapsto\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(X_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g).
Hence, by definition,
ℙ⁡(∫𝒢∏i=1nf¯ξ​(yi,g⁡(xi))​𝑑π~​(g)=0)\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)=0\biggr)
=𝔼⁡{ℙ⁡(∫𝒢∏i=1nfξ​(yi,g⁡(Xi))​𝑑π~​(g)=0|X1,…,Xn)}=0.\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\mathbb{P}\biggl(\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(X_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)=0\,\biggm|\,X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\biggr)\biggr\}=0.
This holds for all n∈[N]n\in[N], which proves the claim.
∎
Lemma 11.
Let d1,d2∈ℕd_{1},d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}, let f:ℝd1→ℝd2f:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}} be Borel measurable and let μ\mu be a Borel probability measure on ℝd1\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}. Then for every ϵ>0\epsilon>0 and δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), there exist d′∈ℕd^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}, W1∈ℝd1×d′W_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d^{\prime}}, W2∈ℝd′×d2W_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}\times d_{2}} and v∈ℝd′v\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}} such that
μ⁡({x∈ℝd1:‖f⁡(x)−W2⊤​ρ​(W1⊤​x+v)‖∞≤ϵ})≥1−δ.\displaystyle\mu\Bigl(\bigl\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}:\bigl\|f(x)-W_{2}^{\top}\rho(W_{1}^{\top}x+v)\bigr\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon\bigr\}\Bigr)\geq 1-\delta.
Proof.
We first prove the claim for d2=1d_{2}=1. Let K0⊆ℝd1K_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}} be a compact set such that μ⁡(K0)≥1−δ/3\mu(K_{0})\geq 1-\delta/3.
By Lusin’s theorem (Folland, 1999, Theorems 7.8 and 7.10), there exists a (compactly supported) continuous function h:ℝd1→ℝh:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\to\mathbb{R} such that μ⁡({x∈K0:f⁡(x)≠h⁡(x)})≤δ/3\mu\bigl(\{x\in K_{0}:f(x)\neq h(x)\}\bigr)\leq\delta/3. Since one-hidden layer neural networks with non-polynomial activation are dense in the set of continuous functions on compact domains equipped with the uniform norm (Leshno et al., 1993, Theorem 1), there exist d′∈ℕd^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}, W1∈ℝd1×d′W_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d^{\prime}}, W2∈ℝd′×d2W_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}\times d_{2}} and v∈ℝd′v\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}} such that for all x∈K0x\in K_{0},
|h⁡(x)−W2⊤​ρ​(W1⊤​x+v)|≤δ​ϵ3.\displaystyle\bigl|h(x)-W_{2}^{\top}\rho(W_{1}^{\top}x+v)\bigr|\leq\frac{\delta\epsilon}{3}.
It follows by Markov’s inequality that
μ({x∈ℝd1:|f(x)\displaystyle\mu\Bigl(\bigl\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}:\bigl|f(x)
−W2⊤ρ(W1⊤x+v)|≥ϵ})\displaystyle-W_{2}^{\top}\rho(W_{1}^{\top}x+v)\bigr|\geq\epsilon\bigr\}\Bigr)
≤μ⁡({x∈K0:|f⁡(x)−W2⊤​ρ​(W1⊤​x+v)|≥ϵ})+δ3\displaystyle\leq\mu\Bigl(\bigl\{x\in K_{0}:\bigl|f(x)-W_{2}^{\top}\rho(W_{1}^{\top}x+v)\bigr|\geq\epsilon\bigr\}\Bigr)+\frac{\delta}{3}
≤μ⁡({x∈K0:|h⁡(x)−W2⊤​ρ​(W1⊤​x+v)|≥ϵ})+δ3+δ3≤δ.\displaystyle\leq\mu\Bigl(\bigl\{x\in K_{0}:\bigl|h(x)-W_{2}^{\top}\rho(W_{1}^{\top}x+v)\bigr|\geq\epsilon\bigr\}\Bigr)+\frac{\delta}{3}+\frac{\delta}{3}\leq\delta.
This proves the claim when d2=1d_{2}=1. For d2>1d_{2}>1, we may approximate the component functions in parallel and apply a union bound to deduce the final result.
∎
Lemma 12.
Let dmodel≥d+2d_{\mathrm{model}}\geq d+2 and let f1:ℝd×ℝ→ℝdmodelf_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{model}}} and f2:ℝd→ℝdmodelf_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{model}}} be Borel measurable. For n∈[N]n\in[N], let Zin(n,dmodel)∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodelZ_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n,d_{\mathrm{model}})}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}} be defined as in (17) and let
U(n)≔(f1⊤​(X1,Y1)f1⊤​(Xn,Yn)f2⊤​(X))∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodel.\displaystyle U^{(n)}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}^{\top}(X_{1},Y_{1})\\
\vdots\\
f_{1}^{\top}(X_{n},Y_{n})\\
f_{2}^{\top}(X)\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times d_{\mathrm{model}}}.
Then for δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1) and ϵ>0\epsilon>0, there exists an FFN layer 𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}} such that with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta,
maxn∈[N]⁡‖𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn​(Zin(n,dmodel))−U(n)‖∞≤ϵ.\displaystyle\max_{n\in[N]}\bigl\|\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}}(Z_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n,d_{\mathrm{model}})})-U^{(n)}\bigr\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon.
Proof.
Define a Borel measurable function f:ℝdmodel→ℝdmodelf:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{model}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{model}}} by f⁡(x,y,𝟎dmodel−d−2,0)≔f1​(x,y)−(x,y,𝟎dmodel−d−2,0)f(x,y,\bm{0}_{d_{\mathrm{model}}-d-2},0)\coloneqq f_{1}(x,y)-(x,y,\bm{0}_{d_{\mathrm{model}}-d-2},0) and f⁡(x,0,𝟎dmodel−d−2,1)≔f2​(x)−(x,0,𝟎dmodel−d−2,1)f(x,0,\bm{0}_{d_{\mathrm{model}}-d-2},1)\coloneqq f_{2}(x)-(x,0,\bm{0}_{d_{\mathrm{model}}-d-2},1) for (x,y)∈ℝd×ℝ(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R} and set ff to be zero otherwise.
Let μ1\mu_{1} denote the distribution of (X,Y)(X,Y) and let μ2\mu_{2} denote the marginal distribution of XX. Define a distribution μ\mu on ℝdmodel\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{model}}} by
μ(∏j=1dmodelAj)≔μ1(∏j=1d+1Aj)𝟙{0∈Admodel}+μ2(∏j=1dAj)𝟙{1∈Admodel}2⋅∏j=d+2dmodel−1𝟙{0∈Aj},\displaystyle\mu\biggl(\prod_{j=1}^{d_{\mathrm{model}}}A_{j}\biggr)\coloneqq\frac{\mu_{1}(\prod_{j=1}^{d+1}A_{j})\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\in A_{d_{\mathrm{model}}}\}}+\mu_{2}(\prod_{j=1}^{d}A_{j})\mathbbm{1}_{\{1\in A_{d_{\mathrm{model}}}\}}}{2}\cdot\prod_{j=d+2}^{{d_{\mathrm{model}}}-1}\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\in A_{j}\}},
for all measurable sets A1,…,Admodel⊆ℝA_{1},\ldots,A_{d_{\mathrm{model}}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}. By Lemma 11 and the definition of μ\mu above, there exists an FFN layer 𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}} such that
ℙ(∥𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\bigl\|\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}}
(X,Y,𝟎dmodel−d−2,0)−f1(X,Y)∥∞>ϵ)\displaystyle(X,Y,\bm{0}_{{d_{\mathrm{model}}}-d-2},0)-f_{1}(X,Y)\bigr\|_{\infty}>\epsilon\Bigr)
+ℙ⁡(‖𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn​(X,0,𝟎dmodel−d−2,1)−f2​(X)‖∞>ϵ)≤δN+1.\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\bigl\|\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}}(X,0,\bm{0}_{{d_{\mathrm{model}}}-d-2},1)-f_{2}(X)\bigr\|_{\infty}>\epsilon\Bigr)\leq\frac{\delta}{N+1}.
We conclude the claim by a union bound.
∎
Proof of Theorem 2.
Let δ≔7​ϵ80​R2∧1\delta\coloneqq\frac{7\epsilon}{80R^{2}}\wedge 1. By Lemma 10 and a union bound, there exists b∈(0,1]b\in(0,1] such that the event
ℰ0≔{minn∈[N]∫𝒢∏i=1nf¯ξ(yi,g(xi))dπ~(g)≥b}\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}\coloneqq\Bigl\{\min_{n\in[N]}\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)\geq b\Bigr\}
has probability at least 1−δ1-\delta.
Let ϵ1≔b8∧b​ϵ64​(R∨1)\epsilon_{1}\coloneqq\frac{b}{8}\wedge\frac{b\sqrt{\epsilon}}{64(R\vee 1)}. By Lemma 9, there exist M≥dM\geq d and g1,…,gM∈𝒢g_{1},\ldots,g_{M}\in\mathcal{G} such that the event
ℰ1≔{maxn∈[N]|1M∑m=1Mgm(X)∏i=1nf¯ξ(Yi,gm(Xi))−∫𝒢g(X)∏i=1nf¯ξ(Yi,g(Xi))dπ~(g)|≤ϵ1}\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\coloneqq\biggl\{\max_{n\in[N]}\biggl|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}g_{m}(X)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},g_{m}(X_{i})\bigr)-\int_{\mathcal{G}}g(X)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},g(X_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)\biggr|\leq\epsilon_{1}\biggr\}
⋂{maxn∈[N]|1M∑m=1M∏i=1nf¯ξ(Yi,gm(Xi))−∫𝒢∏i=1nf¯ξ(Yi,g(Xi))dπ~(g)|≤ϵ1}\displaystyle\bigcap\biggl\{\max_{n\in[N]}\biggl|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},g_{m}(X_{i})\bigr)-\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},g(X_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)\biggr|\leq\epsilon_{1}\biggr\}
has probability at least 1−δ1-\delta.
Now let dmodel≔4​M+1{d_{\mathrm{model}}}\coloneqq 4M+1 and define f1=(f1,1,…,f1,dmodel):ℝd×ℝ→ℝdmodelf_{1}=(f_{1,1},\ldots,f_{1,{d_{\mathrm{model}}}}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{model}}} by
f1,j(x,y)≔{log⁡f¯ξ​(y,gj​(x))if ​j∈[M]log⁡f¯ξ​(y,gj−M​(x))if j∈[M+1:2M]0if j∈[2M+1:4M+1].\displaystyle f_{1,j}(x,y)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\log\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y,g_{j}(x)\bigr)\quad&\text{if }j\in[M]\\
\log\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y,g_{j-M}(x)\bigr)\quad&\text{if }j\in[M+1:2M]\\
0&\text{if }j\in[2M+1:4M+1].\end{cases}
Also define f2=(f2,1,…,f2,dmodel):ℝd→ℝdmodelf_{2}=(f_{2,1},\ldots,f_{2,{d_{\mathrm{model}}}}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{model}}} by
f2,j(x)≔{log⁡gj​(x)if ​j∈[M]0if j∈[M+1:4M]1if ​j=4​M+1.\displaystyle f_{2,j}(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\log g_{j}(x)\quad&\text{if }j\in[M]\\
0&\text{if }j\in[M+1:4M]\\
1&\text{if }j=4M+1.\end{cases}
Finally, for n∈[N]n\in[N], define U(n)∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodelU^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times{d_{\mathrm{model}}}} by
U(n)≔(f1​(X1,Y1)f1​(Xn,Yn)f2​(X)).\displaystyle U^{(n)}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}(X_{1},Y_{1})\\
\vdots\\
f_{1}(X_{n},Y_{n})\\
f_{2}(X)\end{pmatrix}.
By Lemma 12, there exists an FFN layer 𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn(1)\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{(1)}} with θffn(1)=(W1,W2,v)\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{(1)}=(W_{1},W_{2},v), such that writing ϵ2≔b32​(N+1)∧b​ϵ256​R​(N+1)\epsilon_{2}\coloneqq\frac{b}{32(N+1)}\wedge\frac{b\sqrt{\epsilon}}{256R(N+1)}, the event
ℰ2≔{maxn∈[N]∥𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn(1)(Zin(n,dmodel))−U(n)∥∞≤ϵ2}\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}\coloneqq\Bigl\{\max_{n\in[N]}\bigl\|\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{(1)}}(Z_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n,{d_{\mathrm{model}}})})-U^{(n)}\bigr\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon_{2}\Bigr\}
has probability at least 1−δ1-\delta. Since the final column of W2W_{2} only affects the final column of 𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn(1)​(Zin(n,dmodel))\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{(1)}}(Z_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n,{d_{\mathrm{model}}})}), we can choose the final column of W2W_{2} to be zero, so that
[𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn(1)(Zin(n,dmodel))]i,dmodel=[Zin(n,dmodel)]i,dmodel=𝟙{i=n+1}=[U(n)]i,dmodelfor i∈[n+1].\displaystyle\bigl[\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{(1)}}(Z_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n,{d_{\mathrm{model}}})})\bigr]_{i,{d_{\mathrm{model}}}}=\bigl[Z_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n,{d_{\mathrm{model}}})}\bigr]_{i,{d_{\mathrm{model}}}}=\mathbbm{1}_{\{i=n+1\}}=\bigl[U^{(n)}\bigr]_{i,{d_{\mathrm{model}}}}\quad\text{for }i\in[n+1].
(18)
Next, we define Q(2)=K(2)≔𝟎dmodel×dmodelQ^{(2)}=K^{(2)}\coloneqq\bm{0}_{{d_{\mathrm{model}}}\times{d_{\mathrm{model}}}} and define V(2)∈ℝdmodel×dmodelV^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{{d_{\mathrm{model}}}\times{d_{\mathrm{model}}}} by Vj,2​M+j(2)≔1V^{(2)}_{j,2M+j}\coloneqq 1 for j∈[2​M]j\in[2M], V4​M+1,4​M+1(2)≔1V^{(2)}_{4M+1,4M+1}\coloneqq 1 and zero otherwise. Let θattn(2)≔(Q(2),K(2),V(2))\theta^{(2)}_{\mathrm{attn}}\coloneqq(Q^{(2)},K^{(2)},V^{(2)}), Z~(n)≔𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn(1)​(Zin(n,dmodel))∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodel\widetilde{Z}^{(n)}\coloneqq\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{(1)}}(Z_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n,{d_{\mathrm{model}}})})\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times{d_{\mathrm{model}}}} and Z¯(n)≔𝖠𝗍𝗍𝗇θattn(2)​(Z~(n))∈ℝ(n+1)×dmodel\bar{Z}^{(n)}\coloneqq\mathsf{Attn}_{\theta^{(2)}_{\mathrm{attn}}}(\widetilde{Z}^{(n)})\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times{d_{\mathrm{model}}}}. Then by construction and (18), the (n+1)(n+1)th row of Z¯(n)\bar{Z}^{(n)} is given by
Z¯(n)n+1,j={Z~n+1,j(n)if ​j∈[2​M]Z~n+1,j(n)+1n+1​∑i=1n+1Z~i,j−2​M(n)if j∈[2M+1:4M]1+1n+1if ​j=4​M+1.\displaystyle\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,j}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,j}\quad&\text{if }j\in[2M]\\

1+\frac{1}{n+1}\quad&\text{if }j=4M+1.\end{cases}
(19)
Thus, for n∈[N]n\in[N] and m∈[M]m\in[M], we have that on ℰ2\mathcal{E}_{2},
|Z¯(n)n+1,2​M+m−1n+1∑i=1n\displaystyle\biggl|\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,2M+m}-\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}
logf¯ξ(Yi,gm(Xi))−1n+1loggm(X)|\displaystyle\log\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},g_{m}(X_{i})\bigr)-\frac{1}{n+1}\log g_{m}(X)\biggr|
=|Z~n+1,2​M+m(n)+1n+1​∑i=1n+1Z~i,m(n)−1n+1​∑i=1n+1Ui,m(n)|\displaystyle=\biggl|\widetilde{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,2M+m}+\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\widetilde{Z}^{(n)}_{i,m}-\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}U^{(n)}_{i,m}\biggr|
≤|Z~n+1,2​M+m(n)−0|+1n+1​∑i=1n+1|Z~i,m(n)−Ui,m(n)|≤2​ϵ2.\displaystyle\leq\bigl|\widetilde{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,2M+m}-0\bigr|+\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\bigl|\widetilde{Z}^{(n)}_{i,m}-U^{(n)}_{i,m}\bigr|\leq 2\epsilon_{2}.
(20)
Similarly, for n∈[N]n\in[N] and m∈[M]m\in[M], we have that on ℰ2\mathcal{E}_{2},
|Z¯n+1,3​M+m(n)−1n+1​∑i=1nlog⁡f¯ξ​(Yi,gm​(Xi))|≤2​ϵ2.\displaystyle\biggl|\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,3M+m}-\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},g_{m}(X_{i})\bigr)\biggr|\leq 2\epsilon_{2}.
(21)
Now define a continuous function f3:ℝ4​M+1→ℝf_{3}:\mathbb{R}^{4M+1}\to\mathbb{R} by
f3​(z1,…,z4​M+1)≔∑m=1Mexp⁡{z2​M+m/(z4​M+1−1)}∑m=1Mexp⁡{z3​M+m/(z4​M+1−1)}.\displaystyle f_{3}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{4M+1})\coloneqq\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{M}\exp\{z_{2M+m}/(z_{4M+1}-1)\}}{\sum_{m=1}^{M}\exp\{z_{3M+m}/(z_{4M+1}-1)\}}.
For n∈[N]n\in[N], let P¯(n)\bar{P}^{(n)} be the distribution of the last row of Z¯(n)\bar{Z}^{(n)}.
By applying Lemma 11 with μ=1N​∑n=1NP¯(n)\mu=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bar{P}^{(n)} therein, there exists an FFN layer 𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn(2)\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{(2)}} such that the event
ℰ3≔{maxn∈[N]|[𝖥𝖥𝖭θffn(2)(Z¯(n))]n+1,d+1−f3(Z¯n+1,1(n),…,Z¯n+1,dmodel(n))|≤ϵ/4}\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3}\coloneqq\Bigl\{\max_{n\in[N]}\Bigl|\bigl[\mathsf{FFN}_{\theta_{\mathrm{ffn}}^{(2)}}(\bar{Z}^{(n)})\bigr]_{n+1,d+1}-f_{3}\bigl(\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,1},\ldots,\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,{d_{\mathrm{model}}}}\bigr)\Bigr|\leq\sqrt{\epsilon}/4\Bigr\}
has probability at least 1−δ1-\delta.
Since ‖gm‖∞≤R\|g_{m}\|_{\infty}\leq R and ‖f¯ξ‖∞≤1\|\bar{f}_{\xi}\|_{\infty}\leq 1, we have by (19) and (20) that on ℰ1∩ℰ2\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}, for all n∈[N]n\in[N],
|1M​∑m=1Mexp⁡{Z¯n+1,2​M+m(n)/(Z¯n+1,4​M+1(n)−1)}−∫𝒢g⁡(X)​∏i=1nf¯ξ​(yi,g⁡(xi))​𝑑π~​(g)|\displaystyle\biggl|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\exp\bigl\{\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,2M+m}/(\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,4M+1}-1)\bigr\}-\int_{\mathcal{G}}g(X)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)\biggr|

≤1M​∑m∈[M]|exp⁡{(n+1)​Z¯n+1,2​M+m(n)}−gm​(X)​∏i=1nf¯ξ​(Yi,gm​(Xi))|+ϵ1\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m\in[M]}\biggl|\exp\bigl\{(n+1)\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,2M+m}\bigr\}-g_{m}(X)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},g_{m}(X_{i})\bigr)\biggr|+\epsilon_{1}
≤R⁡(e2​(N+1)​ϵ2−1)+ϵ1≤b​ϵ32≕ϵ3.\displaystyle\leq R(e^{2(N+1)\epsilon_{2}}-1)+\epsilon_{1}\leq\frac{b\sqrt{\epsilon}}{32}\eqqcolon\epsilon_{3}.
(22)
Similarly, on ℰ1∩ℰ2\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2} we have by (19) and (21) that for all n∈[N]n\in[N],
|1M∑m=1Mexp{Z¯n+1,3​M+m(n)/(Z¯n+1,4​M+1(n)\displaystyle\biggl|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\exp\bigl\{\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,3M+m}/(\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,4M+1}
−1)}−∫𝒢∏i=1nf¯ξ(yi,g(xi))dπ~(g)|\displaystyle-1)\bigr\}-\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)\biggr|
≤(e2​(N+1)​ϵ2−1)+ϵ1≤b​ϵ32​R∧b4≕ϵ4.\displaystyle\leq(e^{2(N+1)\epsilon_{2}}-1)+\epsilon_{1}\leq\frac{b\sqrt{\epsilon}}{32R}\wedge\frac{b}{4}\eqqcolon\epsilon_{4}.
(23)
Now, on the event ℰ0∩ℰ1\mathcal{E}_{0}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}, since ϵ1≤b/2\epsilon_{1}\leq b/2, we have that
minn∈[N]⁡1M​∑m=1M∏i=1nf¯ξ​(Yi,gm​(Xi))≥b2.\displaystyle\min_{n\in[N]}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(Y_{i},g_{m}(X_{i})\bigr)\geq\frac{b}{2}.
(24)
Therefore, on ℰ0∩ℰ1∩ℰ2\mathcal{E}_{0}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}, we have by (22), (23), (24) and (16) that for all n∈[N]n\in[N]
|f3\displaystyle\bigl|f_{3}
(Z¯n+1,1(n),…,Z¯n+1,dmodel(n))−gπ(X,𝒟n)|\displaystyle(\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,1},\ldots,\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,{d_{\mathrm{model}}}})-g_{\pi}(X,\mathcal{D}_{n})\bigr|
=|∑m=1Mexp⁡{Z¯n+1,2​M+m(n)/(Z¯n+1,4​M+1(n)−1)}∑m=1Mexp⁡{Z¯n+1,3​M+m(n)/(Z¯n+1,4​M+1(n)−1)}−∫𝒢g⁡(X)​∏i=1nf¯ξ​(yi,g⁡(xi))​𝑑π~​(g)∫𝒢∏i=1nf¯ξ​(yi,g⁡(xi))​𝑑π~​(g)|\displaystyle=\Biggl|\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{M}\exp\{\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,2M+m}/(\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,4M+1}-1)\}}{\sum_{m=1}^{M}\exp\{\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,3M+m}/(\bar{Z}^{(n)}_{n+1,4M+1}-1)\}}-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{G}}g(X)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)}{\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)}\Biggr|
≤(11−2​ϵ4/b−1)​|∫𝒢g⁡(X)​∏i=1nf¯ξ​(yi,g⁡(xi))​𝑑π~​(g)∫𝒢∏i=1nf¯ξ​(yi,g⁡(xi))​𝑑π~​(g)|+ϵ3b/2−ϵ4\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{1-2\epsilon_{4}/b}-1\biggr)\Biggl|\frac{\int_{\mathcal{G}}g(X)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)}{\int_{\mathcal{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{f}_{\xi}\bigl(y_{i},g(x_{i})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(g)}\Biggr|+\frac{\epsilon_{3}}{b/2-\epsilon_{4}}
